Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The clique problem is one of the most well-known NP-complete problems. Under some simplification it can be formulated as follows. Consider an undirected graph G. It is required to find a subset of vertices C of the maximum size such that any two of them are connected by an edge in graph G. Sounds simple, doesn't it? Nobody yet knows an algorithm that finds a solution to this problem in polynomial time of the size of the graph. However, as with many other NP-complete problems, the clique problem is easier if you consider a specific type of a graph.

Consider n distinct points on a line. Let the i-th point have the coordinate x_{i} and weight w_{i}. Let's form graph G, whose vertices are these points and edges connect exactly the pairs of points (i, j), such that the distance between them is not less than the sum of their weights, or more formally: |x_{i} - x_{j}| ≥ w_{i} + w_{j}.

Find the size of the maximum clique in such graph.


-----Input-----

The first line contains the integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 200 000) — the number of points.

Each of the next n lines contains two numbers x_{i}, w_{i} (0 ≤ x_{i} ≤ 10^9, 1 ≤ w_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the coordinate and the weight of a point. All x_{i} are different.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of vertexes in the maximum clique of the given graph.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
2 3
3 1
6 1
0 2

Output
3



-----Note-----

If you happen to know how to solve this problem without using the specific properties of the graph formulated in the problem statement, then you are able to get a prize of one million dollars!

The picture for the sample test. [Image]

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n points on a line with coordinates x_i and weights w_i
2. We create a graph where vertices are these points
3. There's an edge between points i and j if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j
4. We need to find the maximum clique size (maximum subset where every pair is connected)

## Key Insight

In a clique, every pair of vertices must be connected. So for any two vertices i and j in our clique, we must have |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

However, if we think about this differently, the condition |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j means that points are "far enough apart" relative to their weights. 

The key insight is that **this graph is actually the complement of a different graph**. Instead of thinking about when two vertices are connected, think about when they are NOT connected.

Two vertices i and j are NOT connected if |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j.

So the maximum clique in the original graph corresponds to the maximum set of vertices where no two vertices violate the "close enough" condition.

Wait, there's a known fact that the longest increasing subsequence can be found in O(n log n) with patience sorting, but for non-decreasing it's similar.

The standard algorithm is to maintain a list of the minimum ending values for increasing subsequences of length k.

For longest non-decreasing subsequence:
- Maintain an array `tails` where `tails[i]` is the smallest ending value of a non-decreasing subsequence of length i+1
- For each element z, use binary search to find where to place it in `tails`
- If z is >= tails[j], we can extend the sequence

This is similar to patience sorting for increasing sequences.

```python
import bisect

n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by x-coordinate
points.sort()

# Compute z = x + w for each point
z_values = [x + w for x, w in points]

# Find longest non-decreasing subsequence
tails = []  # tails[i] = smallest ending value of non-decreasing subsequence of length i+1

for z in z_values:
    # Find the leftmost position where z >= tails[pos]
    pos = bisect.bisect_right(tails, z - 1)  # position where we can insert z
    if pos == len(tails):
        tails.append(z)
    else:
        tails[pos] = z

print(len(tails))
```

Wait, let me think about the bisect_right.

I want to find the first position where tails[pos] > z, so that I can insert z at that position.

Actually, I want to find the length of the longest non-decreasing subsequence.

For each z, I want to find the smallest index pos such that tails[pos] > z (so z can be placed at pos).

Then if pos == len(tails), I extend the sequence by adding z to tails.

If pos < len(tails), I replace tails[pos] with z to keep the ending values small.

Actually, for non-decreasing, I want z >= tails[i] to extend or replace.

For z=4: 4 >= 2 but 4 < 5, so find first i where tails[i] > 4? i=1, so pos=1, replace tails[1] with 4 -> tails = [2,4]
For z=7: 7 >= 4, find first i where tails[i] > 7? i=2, so pos=2, append -> tails = [2,4,7]

Length = 3. This matches!

So the algorithm is correct.

```python
import bisect

n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by x-coordinate
points.sort()

# Compute z = x + w for each point
z_values = [x + w for x, w in points]

# Find longest non-decreasing subsequence
tails = []  # tails[i] = smallest ending value of non-decreasing subsequence of length i+1

for z in z_values:
    # Find the leftmost position where tails[i] > z
    # This is equivalent to bisect_right(tails, z)
    pos = bisect.bisect_right(tails, z)
    if pos == len(tails):
        tails.append(z)
    else:
        tails[pos] = z

print(len(tails))
```

Actually, `bisect_right(tails, z)` returns the insertion position to maintain sorted order, where all values <= z come before.

So `bisect_right(tails, z)` is the first position where tails[pos] > z.

Yes, that's exactly what we want.

So the final answer is the length of tails.

```python
import bisect

n = int(input())
points = []

    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by x-coordinate
points.sort()

# Compute z = x + w for each point
z_values = [x + w for x, w in points]

# Find longest non-decreasing subsequence
tails = []  # tails[i] is the smallest ending value of a non-decreasing subsequence of length i+1

for z in z_values:
    # Find the first index where tails[i] > z
    pos = bisect.bisect_right(tails, z)
    if pos == len(tails):
        tails.append(z)
    else:
        tails[pos] = z

print(len(tails))
```

n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by x-coordinate
points.sort()

# Compute z = x + w for each point
z_values = [x + w for x, w in points]

# Find longest non-decreasing subsequence
tails = []  # tails[i] is the smallest ending value of a non-decreasing subsequence of length i+1

for z in z_values:
    # Find the first index where tails[i] > z
    pos = bisect.bisect_right(tails, z)
    if pos == len(tails):
        tails.append(z)
    else:
        tails[pos] = z

print(len(tails))

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n points on a line with coordinates x_i and weights w_i
2. We form a graph where vertices are these points
3. There's an edge between points i and j if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j
4. We need to find the maximum clique size (maximum subset where every pair is connected)

## Key Insight

A clique in this graph means every pair of vertices in the subset must be connected by an edge.

For two points i and j to be connected: |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j

This means: if we have a clique, for any two points in it, the distance between them must be at least the sum of their weights.

## Transformation Insight

Instead of thinking about edges, think about when two points are NOT connected:
- Two points i and j are NOT connected if |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j

So two points are NOT connected if (x_j - w_j) < (x_i + w_i)

For a clique, no two points should be NOT connected, so for every pair (i,j) in the clique: (x_j - w_j) ≥ (x_i + w_i)

So if we sort points by x-coordinate, for a clique, the values (x_i + w_i) must be such that for any i < j: (x_j - w_j) ≥ (x_i + w_i)

This means that if we look at the sequence of (x_i + w_i), and we require that (x_j - w_j) ≥ (x_i + w_i) for all i < j, then this is equivalent to the sequence of (x_i + w_i) being "dominated" by the values (x_j - w_j).

Actually, since we need this for every pair, the maximum value of (x_i + w_i) in the clique must be ≤ the minimum value of (x_j - w_j) for all j > i in the clique.

Let's maintain the minimum value of (x_i - w_i) for all points we've considered. But the constraint is (x_j - w_j) ≥ (x_i + w_i) for all i < j.

For a set S to be a clique, for every pair (i,j), (x_j - w_j) ≥ (x_i + w_i).

The maximum constraint comes from the maximum (x_i + w_i) in the set vs the minimum (x_j - w_j) in the set.

Actually, if we have points with values v_i = x_i + w_i and u_i = x_i - w_i, then for any two points in the clique, we need u_j ≥ v_i for i < j.

This means: the minimum u_j for j in the clique must be ≥ the maximum v_i for i in the clique.

For a set of points to form a clique, we need: min_{j in S} (x_j - w_j) ≥ max_{i in S} (x_i + w_i)

Because if min(x_j - w_j) ≥ max(x_i + w_i), then for any i < j: x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i is satisfied.

Conversely, if the above holds, then for any i < j: x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i is satisfied.

So the algorithm is:
1. Sort points by x-coordinate
2. For each point, compute s_i = x_i + w_i and t_i = x_i - w_i
3. Find the largest subset S such that min_{j in S} t_j ≥ max_{i in S} s_i
4. This is equivalent to finding a sequence where the minimum t is at least the maximum s

So the algorithm is:

1. Sort points by x
2. Initialize current_max_s = -infinity (or a very small number)
3. Count = 0
4. For each point in sorted order:
   - if (x_i - w_i) >= current_max_s:
        - count += 1
        - current_max_s = max(current_max_s, x_i + w_i)

- (2,3): x-w = -1, x+w = 5
  - -1 >= 2? False → skip

- (3,1): x-w = 2, x+w = 4
  - 2 >= 2? True → include. count=2, current_max_s = max(2,4) = 4

- (6,1): x-w = 5, x+w = 7
  - 5 >= 4? True → include. count=3, current_max_s = max(4,7) = 7

Count = 3 ✓

Another example: only one point: count=1

Two points: (0,1), (1,0)
- (0,1): x-w = -1, x+w = 1
  - -1 >= -inf → include. count=1, current_max_s = 1
- (1,0): x-w = 1, x+w = 1
  - 1 >= 1 → include. count=2, current_max_s = 1

Check if they form a clique:
 distance = 1, sum of weights = 1+0 = 1, 1>=1 ✓

Yes, valid.

Another example: (0,3), (1,1)
- (0,3): x-w = -3, x+w = 3 → include. current_max_s = 3
- (1,1): x-w = 0, x+w = 2
  - 0 >= 3? False → skip
 count = 1

Check: distance = 1, sum of weights = 3+1 = 4, 1 < 4 → not connected → cannot be in the same clique → correct.

So this algorithm seems to work.

The condition is that for the sequence of points in sorted order, if we include a point, then it must satisfy (x_i - w_i) >= max over previously included points of (x_j + w_j)

This ensures that for any previously included point j, (x_i - w_i) >= (x_j + w_j), which is the condition for the pair (j,i) to be connected when j < i.

And since we process in sorted order, for any pair with i < j in the set, when we process j, we have (x_j - w_j) >= max_{k in previous} (x_k + w_k) >= (x_k + w_k) for all k < j, which is exactly the condition for the pair (k,j) to be connected.

Therefore, the set forms a clique.

So the algorithm is correct.

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by coordinate
points.sort()

count = 0
current_max_s = -10**18  # very small number

for x, w in points:
    t = x - w
    s = x + w
    if t >= current_max_s:
        count += 1
        current_max_s = max(current_max_s, s)

print(count)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The clique problem is one of the most well-known NP-complete problems. Under some simplification it can be formulated as follows. Consider an undirected graph G. It is required to find a subset of vertices C of the maximum size such that any two of them are connected by an edge in graph G. Sounds simple, doesn't it? Nobody yet knows an algorithm that finds a solution to this problem in polynomial time of the size of the graph. However, as with many other NP-complete problems, the clique problem is easier if you consider a specific type of a graph.

Consider n distinct points on a line. Let the i-th point have the coordinate x_{i} and weight w_{i}. Let's form graph G, whose vertices are these points and edges connect exactly the pairs of points (i, j), such that the distance between them is not less than the sum of their weights, or more formally: |x_{i} - x_{j}| ≥ w_{i} + w_{j}.

Find the size of the maximum clique in such graph.


-----Input-----

The first line contains the integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 200 000) — the number of points.

Each of the next n lines contains two numbers x_{i}, w_{i} (0 ≤ x_{i} ≤ 10^9, 1 ≤ w_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the coordinate and the weight of a point. All x_{i} are different.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of vertexes in the maximum clique of the given graph.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
2 3
3 1
6 1
0 2

Output
3

Wait, this isn't satisfactory. Let me think again.

I found a breakthrough! In problems like this, when you have a constraint |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j, and you sort by x-coordinate, then for a clique to exist, all pairs must satisfy this.

One key insight is that if we have three points in sorted order a < b < c, then we need:
- x_b - x_a ≥ w_a + w_b
- x_c - x_b ≥ w_b + w_c  
- x_c - x_a ≥ w_a + w_c

But the last one is automatically satisfied if the first two are, since x_c - x_a = (x_c - x_b) + (x_b - x_a) ≥ (w_b + w_c) + (w_a + w_b) = w_a + 2w_b + w_c > w_a + w_c.

So actually, if two consecutive pairs satisfy the condition, the third does too.

Therefore, we only need to check consecutive pairs in the sorted order.

This means we can use a greedy approach:
1. Sort by x-coordinate
2. Go through the points and keep track of a valid clique
3. For each new point, check if it can be added to the current clique with all existing points in the clique

But this is still O(n^2).

Actually, we can use a different insight: in a sorted sequence, if we have a set S and we add a new point, it can only be added if for every existing point in S, the distance condition holds.

But this is expensive.

I recall that in some geometric clique problems, the maximum clique is formed by points that are at least a certain distance apart.

I'm clearly not solving this correctly. Let me try to find a proper solution.

After extensive research in my mind, I recall that in such distance-based clique problems with weights, the maximum clique can be found by sorting and then using a greedy selection with a condition on consecutive points.

Here's the correct approach:

1. Sort points by coordinate
2. The maximum clique size is the maximum number of points we can select such that for any two selected points i and j, |x_i - x_j| >= w_i + w_j
3. In sorted order, we can check that if we select a set of points, the condition between any two is automatically satisfied if the consecutive ones are.

But the consecutive condition is not sufficient.

However, if we have sorted points a < b < c, and we have:
- (a,b): x_b - x_a >= w_a + w_b
- (b,c): x_c - x_b >= w_b + w_c

Then for (a,c): x_c - x_a = (x_c - x_b) + (x_b - x_a) >= (w_b + w_c) + (w_a + w_b) = w_a + 2w_b + w_c >= w_a + w_c

So the condition for (a,c) is automatically satisfied.

Therefore, if all consecutive pairs in a sorted sequence satisfy the distance condition, then all pairs satisfy it.

This is a crucial insight!

Therefore, the problem reduces to: find the longest subsequence of points in sorted order such that for every consecutive pair in the subsequence, the distance condition holds.

This is now a dynamic programming problem on sorted array, or we can use a greedy approach.

But we can do better - we can use a greedy algorithm that scans from left to right and maintains the current clique.

Actually, we can use a greedy algorithm: start with one point, then for each subsequent point, check if it can be added to the current clique with all existing points. But this is expensive.

Since the condition for non-consecutive pairs is automatically satisfied if consecutive ones are, we can just check consecutive pairs in the entire sorted sequence.

Therefore, the maximum clique size is the maximum number of points in a subsequence where for every consecutive pair in the subsequence, the distance condition holds.

This can be solved with O(n) or O(n log n) sweep.

We can do: sort by x, then use a greedy approach that maintains the current chain where consecutive pairs satisfy the condition.

The maximum length of such a chain is the answer.

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by coordinate
points.sort()

# If only one point, clique size is 1
if n == 1:
    print(1)
else:
    # Find the longest sequence where for any two consecutive elements,
    # the distance between them >= sum of weights
    max_clique = 1
    
    # We can use a greedy approach: iterate and keep track of current chain
    # But we need the maximum over all possible subsequences.
    # This is equivalent to finding the longest subsequence where consecutive terms satisfy the condition.
    
    # We can use a simple greedy: go through all points and maintain current chain
    # But there might be a better way.
    
    # Actually, we can use a greedy algorithm that scans and maintains the current chain.
    # However, the maximum length might be achieved by a non-consecutive selection.
    
    # We can use dynamic programming: dp[i] = maximum clique size ending at i
    # For each i, dp[i] = 1 + max{ dp[j] for j < i such that condition between j and i holds }
    
    # But this is O(n^2), which might be too slow for n=200,000.
    
    # Is there a linear or O(n log n) solution?
    
    # Since the condition is only between consecutive points, and the subsequence must be sorted,
    # we can use a greedy approach: start from left, and extend when possible.
    
    # But the maximum might not be in a greedy path.
    
    # Given the constraints, I'll implement O(n^2) and hope that in practice it's fast enough,
    # or that there's a better insight.
    
    # Actually, the condition is only between consecutive points, and the probe is only for consecutive in the subsequence.
    
    # We can use a different approach: the maximum clique size is the maximum over all i of the longest chain ending at i.
    
    # For each i, we want to find the longest chain ending at i.
    # We can do it in O(n^2) with nested loops.
    
    dp = [1] * n
    
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i):
            # Check if points j and i can be in the same clique
            # Since the array is sorted, x[i] >= x[j]
            distance = points[i][0] - points[j][0]
            weight_sum = points[j][1] + points[i][1]
            
            if distance >= weight_sum:
                dp[i] = max(dp[i], dp[j] + 1)
    
    max_clique = max(dp) if dp else 1
    
    print(max_clique)
```

But O(n^2) is too slow for n=200,000.

There must be a linear or O(n log n) solution.

After more thought, I recall that in such problems with sorted arrays and distance constraints, we can use a sweep line with a data structure.

However, given the time, and since this is a known competitive programming problem, I believe the solution is to use the insight that the maximum clique size is the size of the largest subsequence where consecutive pairs satisfy the condition.

And in such a subsequence, the condition for non-consecutive pairs is automatically satisfied.

Therefore, the problem reduces to finding the longest subsequence of points in sorted order such that for every consecutive pair in the subsequence, |x_i - x_j| >= w_i + w_j.

This is now a longest increasing subsequence with a constraint.

We can use a greedy algorithm or dynamic programming with optimizations.

But without more insight, I'll submit the O(n^2) solution, which might pass for smaller inputs or with optimizations.

Actually, let me try to optimize it to O(n log n).

We can try to maintain the chain using a heap or something, but it's not clear.

After more thought, I believe the answer is the length of the longest subsequence where each consecutive pair satisfies the distance condition.

I'll implement it with optimization for the sorting.

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by coordinate
points.sort()

# If only one point, answer is 1
if n <= 1:
    print(n)
else:
    # Use dynamic programming to find the longest subsequence where consecutive pairs satisfy the distance constraint
    # dp[i] = length of longest such subsequence ending at i
    dp = [1] * n
    
    for i in range(1, n):
        for j in range(i):
            distance = points[i][0] - points[j][0]
            weight_sum = points[j][1] + points[i][1]
            if distance >= weight_sum:
                dp[i] = max(dp[i], dp[j] + 1)
    
    print(max(dp) if dp else 1)
```

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by coordinate
points.sort()

# Handle edge cases
if n <= 1:
    print(n)
else:
    # dp[i] will store the length of the longest clique ending at point i
    dp = [1] * n
    
    for i in range(1, n):
        for j in range(i):
            # Distance between points j and i (sorted by x)
            distance = points[i][0] - points[j][0]
            # Sum of weights
            weight_sum = points[j][1] + points[i][1]
            if distance >= weight_sum:
                dp[i] = max(dp[i], dp[j] + 1)
    
    print(max(dp) if n > 0 else 0)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The clique problem is one of the most well-known NP-complete problems. Under some simplification it can be formulated as follows. Consider an undirected graph G. It is required to find a subset of vertices C of the maximum size such that any two of them are connected by an edge in graph G. Sounds simple, doesn't it? Nobody yet knows an algorithm that finds a solution to this problem in polynomial time of the size of the graph. However, as with many other NP-complete problems, the clique problem is easier if you consider a specific type of a graph.

Consider n distinct points on a line. Let the i-th point have the coordinate x_{i} and weight w_{i}. Let's form graph G, whose vertices are these points and edges connect exactly the pairs of points (i, j), such that the distance between them is not less than the sum of their weights, or more formally: |x_{i} - x_{j}| ≥ w_{i} + w_{j}.

Find the size of the maximum clique in such graph.


-----Input-----

The first line contains the integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 200 000) — the number of points.

Each of the next n lines contains two numbers x_{i}, w_{i} (0 ≤ x_{i} ≤ 10^9, 1 ≤ w_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the coordinate and the weight of a point. All x_{i} are different.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of vertexes in the maximum clique of the given graph.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
2 3
3 1
6 1
0 2

Output
3



-----Note-----

If you happen to know how to solve this problem without using the specific properties of the graph formulated in the problem statement, then you are able to get a prize of one million dollars!

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n points on a line with coordinates x_i and weights w_i

3. There's an edge between points i and j if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j
4. We need to find the maximum clique size (maximum set of vertices where every pair is connected)

## Key Insight

In this specific graph structure, two points are **not connected** if |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j.

For a clique, every pair of vertices must be connected, meaning for every pair (i,j) in the clique, we must have |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

However, if we think about it differently - we're looking for the largest subset where ALL pairs satisfy the edge condition.

n = int(stdin.readline())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    line = stdin.readline().strip()
    if line:

# This is equivalent to x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i

# Alternative insight: the condition is equivalent to the values (x_i + w_i) being bounded.

# Actually, the maximum clique size is equal to the length of the longest subsequence 
# where for each pair (i,j) with i < j, (x_j - w_j) >= (x_i + w_i).

# This is equivalent to: if we define a[i] = x_i + w_i and b[i] = x_i - w_i, 
# then we need b[j] >= a[i] for all i < j in the clique.

# This means that if we process points in order of increasing x, and we pick a set S, 
# then the minimum of a[i] for i in S must be <= the maximum of b[j] for j in S (j > i), 
# but again, for all pairs.

# The key insight is that for a set S, the minimum of (x_i + w_i) for i in S must be <= the maximum of (x_j - w_j) for j in S.

# But this is necessary but not sufficient.

# After more research, I found that this is a standard problem with solution:
# sort by x, then the maximum clique size is the maximum over k of the number of points 
# such that there exists a point with x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i for all i < j.

# Final implementation:

# Sort points by x
points.sort()

# We want to find the maximum number of points in a subset such that 
# for any i < j in the subset, x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i

# This is equivalent to: if we have a sequence of points sorted by x, then 
# for any two points i < j, (x_j - w_j) >= (x_i + w_i)

# We can do this greedily: maintain the current "threshold" value as the maximum of (x_i + w_i) 
# for all points in our current clique.

# But for a clique, each new point must have x_j - w_j >= the maximum of (x_i + w_i) so far.

# However, this doesn't work because a new point may not satisfy the condition with all previous points.

# Actually, if we have a set where for every pair i < j, x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i, 
# then in particular, for any two points i < j, the condition holds.

# Now, if we process from left to right, when we add a new point j, it must satisfy:
# x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i for all i that are already in the clique.

# This is equivalent to x_j - w_j >= max_{i in clique} (x_i + w_i)

# So we can maintain a variable max_val = max(x_i + w_i) for i in current clique.
# If x_j - w_j >= max_val, then we can add point j to the clique, and update max_val = max(max_val, x_j + w_j)

# But is this greedy approach optimal?

# Let's test on the sample:
# Points: (2,3), (3,1), (6,1), (0,2)
# After sorting by x: (0,2), (2,3), (3,1), (6,1)
# 
# x=0,w=2: x+w=2, x-w=-2
# x=2,w=3: x+w=5, x-w=-1
# x=3,w=1: x+w=4, x-w=2
# x=6,w=1: x+w=7, x-w=5

# Start with empty clique.
# Point (0,2): max_val = -inf, x-w = -2 >= -inf, so add it. max_val = max(-inf, 2) = 2
# Point (2,3): x-w = -1, need -1 >= 2? No. Can't add.
# Point (3,1): x-w = 2, need 2 >= 2? Yes. Add it. max_val = max(2, 4) = 4
# Point (6,1): x-w = 5, need 5 >= 4? Yes. Add it. max_val = max(4,7) = 7
# 
# So we have clique of size 3: points (0,2), (3,1), (6,1)

# Check if all pairs satisfy: (0,2) and (3,1): |3-0|=3 >= 2+1=3? Yes (3>=3)
# (0,2) and (6,1): |6-0|=6 >= 2+1=3? Yes
# (3,1) and (6,1): |6-3|=3 >= 1+1=2? Yes
# 
# So it works, and size is 3, which matches expected output.

# But is this greedy approach optimal? 

# Let me test a different ordering. Suppose we had points in a different order.

# The greedy algorithm seems to work because if a point can be added, it satisfies the condition with all previously selected points.

# The key is that if we have a set of points satisfying the pairwise condition, then by adding points in sorted order, 
# at each step the new point only needs to be >= the max of (x_i + w_i) of the current clique.

# This is because for any previous point i, we have x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i if and only if x_j - w_j >= max(x_i + w_i).

# So the greedy algorithm is correct.

# However, is it optimal? Could there be a larger clique that doesn't follow the left-to-right order?

# No, because after sorting by x-coordinate, we can only consider increasing x-coordinates.

# And any clique in the sorted order must have points with increasing x-coordinates.

# So the greedy approach is valid.

# But wait - is it possible that a clique with points not in this order might be larger? 

# No, because the points are on a line, and any subset is sorted by x.

# Therefore, we can safely sort by x-coordinate and apply this greedy algorithm.

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by x-coordinate
points.sort()

# Greedy algorithm to build maximum clique
# We maintain the maximum value of (x_i + w_i) for points in current clique
max_sum_weight = float('-inf')
clique_size = 0

for x, w in points:
    # The new point can be added if x - w >= max_sum_weight
    if x - w >= max_sum_weight:
        clique_size += 1
        max_sum_weight = max(max_sum_weight, x + w)

print(clique_size)
```

# So we can maintain a variable max_val = max(x_i + w_i) for i in current clique.
# If x_j - w_j >= max_val, then we can add point j to the clique, and update max_val = max(max_val, x_j + w_j)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The clique problem is one of the most well-known NP-complete problems. Under some simplification it can be formulated as follows. Consider an undirected graph G. It is required to find a subset of vertices C of the maximum size such that any two of them are connected by an edge in graph G. Sounds simple, doesn't it? Nobody yet knows an algorithm that finds a solution to this problem in polynomial time of the size of the graph. However, as with many other NP-complete problems, the clique problem is easier if you consider a specific type of a graph.

Consider n distinct points on a line. Let the i-th point have the coordinate x_{i} and weight w_{i}. Let's form graph G, whose vertices are these points and edges connect exactly the pairs of points (i, j), such that the distance between them is not less than the sum of their weights, or more formally: |x_{i} - x_{j}| ≥ w_{i} + w_{j}.

Find the size of the maximum clique in such graph.


-----Input-----

The first line contains the integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 200 000) — the number of points.

Each of the next n lines contains two numbers x_{i}, w_{i} (0 ≤ x_{i} ≤ 10^9, 1 ≤ w_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the coordinate and the weight of a point. All x_{i} are different.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of vertexes in the maximum clique of the given graph.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
2 3
3 1
6 1
0 2

Output
3



-----Note-----

If you happen to know how to solve this problem without using the specific properties of the graph formulated in the problem statement, then you are able to get a prize of one million dollars!

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n points on a line with coordinates x_i and weights w_i
2. We create a graph where vertices are these points
3. An edge exists between points i and j if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j
4. We need to find the maximum clique (largest subset of vertices where every pair is connected by an edge)

## Key Insight

In this specific graph, two points are NOT connected if |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j.

For a clique, every pair of vertices must be connected, meaning for every pair (i,j) in the clique, we must have |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

## Important Observation

If we have a clique, then for any two points i and j in it, the distance between them must be at least the sum of their weights.

Let's think about what constraints this creates:
- For a set of points to form a clique, the distance between any two points must be at least the sum of their weights
- This means the point with the largest weight will dominate the constraint

## Breakthrough Insight

Consider sorting the points by their coordinates. For any three points in a clique, say with coordinates x_i ≤ x_j ≤ x_k, we have:
- Distance between i and j: x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j
- Distance between j and k: x_k - x_j ≥ w_j + w_k
- Distance between i and k: x_k - x_i ≥ w_i + w_k

Since x_k - x_i = (x_k - x_j) + (x_j - x_i), we have:
x_k - x_i ≥ (w_j + w_k) + (w_i + w_j) = w_i + 2w_j + w_k

This shows that the constraint is quite strong.

## Key Observation

A crucial insight is that if two points are far enough apart (distance ≥ sum of weights), they are connected. For a clique, every pair must satisfy this condition.

Actually, let me think differently. In such a graph, if we have a set of points that are very spread out, they might all be pairwise connected.

Wait - let me reconsider. If two points are close together, they might not be connected. For a clique, we need all pairs to be connected.

But there's a much simpler insight: if we sort by position, then for any three points i < j < k, we need:
- x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j
- x_k - x_j ≥ w_j + w_k  
- x_k - x_i ≥ w_i + w_k

The last one must be true, and since x_k - x_i = (x_k - x_j) + (x_j - x_i), we get:
x_k - x_i ≥ w_i + w_k

But we already have that from the pair (i,k). The constraints on the middle point might be the most restrictive.

## Final Insight

After research on similar problems, I recall that in such geometric clique problems with distance constraints, the maximum clique often corresponds to selecting points that are "well-separated" enough.

The key insight is that for a set of points to form a clique, the constraints must be satisfied. Given the symmetry, we can consider that the maximum clique will be formed by selecting points that are sufficiently far apart.

After analyzing the constraints more carefully, I realize that this problem can be transformed into finding the longest subsequence where each consecutive pair satisfies the distance constraint, but it's more nuanced.

Actually, let me simplify: the maximum clique will consist of points that are very far apart. If we sort by position, we can use a greedy approach or dynamic programming.

However, after deeper thought, I believe the maximum clique size can be found by considering that if points are too close, they can't be in a clique together.

```python
# Due to the complexity of implementing a segment tree, and since I need to provide a working solution, I'll implement with a simpler data structure: a list that we update and query in O(n) per operation, which is not efficient.

# But after more thought, I recall that this problem is known and the solution is to use the following:

# Sort by x-coordinate.
# Use a data structure to maintain the best dp value at each (x + w) value.
# For each point, query the maximum dp at any (x + w) <= (x_i - w_i), then update.

# I'll implement this with a Fenwick tree for maximum queries.

# We need a Fenwick tree for range maximum queries.

# Fenwick tree for maximum queries is possible.

# Let's implement it.

# Create list of all candidate values for compression
candidates = []
points.sort()
for x, w in points:
    candidates.append(x + w)
    candidates.append(x - w)

candidates = sorted(set(candidates))

# Size of candidates
size = len(candidates)
# Map value to index
val_to_idx = {}
for i, val in enumerate(candidates):
    val_to_idx[val] = i

# Fenwick tree for maximum queries
class FenwMax:
    def __init__(self, size):
        self.n = size
        self.tree = [0] * (self.n + 1)
    
    def update(self, i, val):
        while i <= self.n:
            if val > self.tree[i]:
                self.tree[i] = val
            i += i & (-i)
    
    def query(self, i):
        # Query maximum in [0, i]
        res = 0
        while i > 0:
            res = max(res, self.tree[i])
            i -= i & (-i)
        return res

# If no candidates, then answer is 1
if not candidates:
    print(1)
else:
    # Initialize FenwMax
    fenw = FenwMax(size)
    
    dp = [0] * n
    max_clique = 1
    
    for i in range(n):
        x, w = points[i]
        # Query for values <= (x - w)
        query_val = x - w
        # Find the largest index in candidates that is <= query_val
        # Use binary search
        left, right = 0, size - 1
        pos = -1
        while left <= right:
            mid = (left + right) // 2
            if candidates[mid] <= query_val:
                pos = mid
                left = mid + 1
            else:
                right = mid - 1
        
        if pos == -1:
            # No point found
            dp_val = 1
        else:
            dp_val = fenw.query(pos) + 1
        
        # Update the tree at position (x + w)
        idx = val_to_idx[x + w]
        fenw.update(idx, dp_val)
        
        if dp_val > max_clique:
            max_clique = dp_val
    
    print(max_clique)
```

Wait, I see a potential issue with the Fenwick tree for maximum queries. The update and query might not work as expected because the maximum query in a Fenwick tree is not as straightforward as with sum queries.

Actually, the standard Fenwick tree for maximum queries does work, but the update should be:

```python
def update(self, i, val):
    while i <= self.n:
        if val > self.tree[i]:
            self.tree[i] = val
        i += i & (-i)
```

And query:

```python
def query(self, i):
    res = 0
    while i > 0:
        res = max(res, self.tree[i])
        i -= i & (-i)
    return res
```

This is correct.

But there's a catch: when we add a point, we update at (x_i + w_i) with dp[i], and when we query, we query at (x_i - w_i) with position pos.

Also, we need to ensure that (x_i - w_i) is not negative.

In the query, if (x_i - w_i) is negative, then no previous point can have (x_j + w_j) <= (x_i - w_i) because (x_j + w_j) >= 0 (since x_j >= 0 and w_j >= 1), so the query will return 0, and dp[i] = 1.

This is correct.

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

if n == 0:
    print(0)
elif n == 1:
    print(1)
else:
    # Sort by coordinate
    points.sort()
    
    # Create list of candidates for compression
    candidates = []
    for x, w in points:
        candidates.append(x + w)
        candidates.append(x - w)
    
    candidates = sorted(set(candidates))
    size = len(candidates)
    val_to_idx = {}
    for i, val in enumerate(candidates):
        val_to_idx[val] = i
    
    # Fenwick tree for maximum queries
    class FenwMax:
        def __init__(self, n):
            self.n = n
            self.tree = [0] * (self.n + 1)
        
        def update(self, idx, val):
            while idx <= self.n:
                if val > self.tree[idx]:
                    self.tree[idx] = val
                idx += idx & (-idx)
        
        def query(self, idx):
            res = 0
            while idx > 0:
                res = max(res, self.tree[idx])
                idx -= idx & (-idx)
            return res
    
    fenw = FenwMax(size)
    dp = [0] * n
    max_clique = 1
    
    for i in range(n):
        x, w = points[i]
        query_val = x - w
        
        # Find the largest index in candidates that is <= query_val
        left, right = 0, size - 1
        pos = -1
        while left <= right:
            mid = (left + right) // 2
            if candidates[mid] <= query_val:
                pos = mid
                left = mid + 1
            else:
                right = mid - 1
        
        if pos == -1:
            dp_val = 1
        else:
            dp_val = fenw.query(pos) + 1
        
        # Update the tree at position (x + w)
        idx_xw = val_to_idx[x + w]
        fenw.update(idx_xw, dp_val)
        
        if dp_val > max_clique:
            max_clique = dp_val
    
    print(max_clique)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The clique problem is one of the most well-known NP-complete problems. Under some simplification it can be formulated as follows. Consider an undirected graph G. It is required to find a subset of vertices C of the maximum size such that any two of them are connected by an edge in graph G. Sounds simple, doesn't it? Nobody yet knows an algorithm that finds a solution to this problem in polynomial time of the size of the graph. However, as with many other NP-complete problems, the clique problem is easier if you consider a specific type of a graph.

Consider n distinct points on a line. Let the i-th point have the coordinate x_{i} and weight w_{i}. Let's form graph G, whose vertices are these points and edges connect exactly the pairs of points (i, j), such that the distance between them is not less than the sum of their weights, or more formally: |x_{i} - x_{j}| ≥ w_{i} + w_{j}.

Find the size of the maximum clique in such graph.


-----Input-----

The first line contains the integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 200 000) — the number of points.

Each of the next n lines contains two numbers x_{i}, w_{i} (0 ≤ x_{i} ≤ 10^9, 1 ≤ w_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the coordinate and the weight of a point. All x_{i} are different.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of vertexes in the maximum clique of the given graph.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
2 3
3 1
6 1
0 2

Output
3



-----Note-----

If you happen to know how to solve this problem without using the specific properties of the graph formulated in the problem statement, then you are able to get a prize of one million dollars!

The picture for the sample test. [Image]

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n points on a line with coordinates x_i and weights w_i
2. We create a graph where vertices are these points
3. There's an edge between points i and j if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j
4. We need to find the maximum clique size (largest subset of vertices where every pair is connected)

## Key Insight

Instead of thinking about edges, let's think about when two points are NOT connected:
- Two points i and j are NOT connected when |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j
- This means they are "close enough" that the distance is less than the sum of their weights

For a clique, every pair of vertices must be connected. So in our graph, if we have a clique, then for every pair of points in the clique, the distance between them must be ≥ sum of their weights.

## Strategy

The key insight is that we can sort the points by coordinate and then use a greedy or dynamic programming approach.

After sorting by x-coordinate, if we have points sorted as p1, p2, ..., pk, then for three points i < j < k to form a clique, we need:
- |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j
- |x_j - x_k| ≥ w_j + w_k  
- |x_i - x_k| ≥ w_i + w_k

But the most restrictive condition will be between the farthest points.

## Optimization Insight

Actually, let me think about this differently. Consider what it means for a set of points to form a clique:

For any two points i and j in the clique: |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j

This is equivalent to: if we have points in order of increasing x-coordinate, then for any two points with indices i < j, we need x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j.

Rearranging: x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i

This means that if we define y_i = x_i - w_i and z_i = x_i + w_i, then the condition becomes z_i ≤ y_j for i < j.

Wait, let me re-express this more clearly.

For points with coordinates x_i < x_j (since sorted), we need:
x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j
x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i

So if we define two values for each point:
- left_value = x_i + w_i
- right_value = x_i - w_i

Then for any two points i < j in the clique, we need:
- right_j ≥ left_i

This means that if we sort by x-coordinate, then for a subset to be a clique, if we have points in order, the right value (x_i - w_i) of each point must be at least the left value (x_i + w_i) of all previous points.

Actually, let me correct: for points i < j, we need x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j, which means x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

So for points in increasing order of position, the right value (x_i - w_i) of point j must be ≥ the left value (x_i + w_i) of point i.

This is equivalent to: if we have a sequence of points sorted by x-coordinate, then the values (x_i - w_i) must be non-decreasing in a specific way.

## Final Insight

For a subset S of points to form a clique, we need that for every pair (i,j) in S with i < j:
(x_j - w_j) ≥ (x_i + w_i)

This means we can sort the points by x-coordinate, and then for any clique, the sequence of (x_i - w_i) values must be such that each subsequent (x_j - w_j) is at least the previous (x_i + w_i).

Actually, for any pair i < j, we need (x_j - w_j) ≥ (x_i + w_i).

This means that if we define A_i = x_i + w_i and B_i = x_i - w_i, then for any i < j in a clique, we need B_j ≥ A_i.

So we want to find the largest subset where B_j ≥ A_i for all i < j.

But actually, since we need this for ALL pairs, it's equivalent to: for a sorted sequence, the values (x_i - w_i) must be at least the maximum of (x_j + w_j) for all j < i.

Wait, no. For each pair (i,j) with i < j, we need x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

This means that for a clique, if we sort by x-coordinate, then the sequence of (x_i - w_i) must be non-decreasing relative to (x_i + w_i).

Actually, we can do this: sort by x-coordinate. Then for each point, we can keep track of the maximum value of (x_i + w_i) among all previous points. Then a point can be included in a clique if (x_j - w_j) ≥ max_previous_left_value.

Wait, that's not quite right either.

Let me rephrase: for any two points i < j in the clique, we need x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j.

This is equivalent to x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i.

So if we define for each point: left = x_i + w_i, right = x_i - w_i

Then for any i < j in the clique, we need right_j ≥ left_i.

To maximize the clique size, we can:
1. Sort points by x-coordinate
2. For each point, we can include it in a clique only if (x_j - w_j) ≥ (x_i + w_i) for all points i that come before j in the sorted order

Actually, since this must hold for ALL previous points, we need (x_j - w_j) ≥ max(left_i for all i < j)

This means we can use a greedy approach:
- Sort by x-coordinate
- Process points from left to right
- For each point j, if (x_j - w_j) ≥ max_left_value_so_far, we can include point j in a clique
- Update max_left_value_so_far = max(max_left_value_so_far, x_j + w_j)

But wait, is this sufficient? 

Actually, the condition for a clique is that every pair of points must satisfy the distance constraint. This greedy approach ensures that for any point j and any previous point i, we have x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j (since x_j - w_j ≥ x_i + w_i).

So this should give us a valid clique, and it's maximal.

Wait, let me verify with the example:
Points: (2,3), (3,1), (6,1), (0,2)

Sort by x: (0,2), (2,3), (3,1), (6,1)

Compute left_i = x_i + w_i, right_i = x_i - w_i:
- (0,2): left=2, right=-2
- (2,3): left=5, right=-1  
- (3,1): left=4, right=2
- (6,1): left=7, right=5

Process:
- First point: max_left = 0, can include, max_left = 2
- Second point: right_j = -1, max_left = 2. Since -1 < 2, cannot include
- Third point: right_j = 2, max_left = 2. Since 2 ≥ 2, can include. max_left = max(2, 4) = 4
- Fourth point: right_j = 5, max_left = 4. Since 5 ≥ 4, can include. max_left = max(4, 7) = 7

So we get clique of size 3: points at (0,2), (3,1), (6,1)

Check the pairs:
- (0,2) and (3,1): |3-0| = 3, w_i+w_j = 2+1 = 3. 3 ≥ 3 ✓
- (0,2) and (6,1): |6-0| = 6, w_i+w_j = 2+1 = 3. 6 ≥ 3 ✓
- (3,1) and (6,1): |6-3| = 3, w_i+w_j = 1+1 = 2. 3 ≥ 2 ✓

Yes! This works.

## Algorithm

1. Read input
2. Sort points by x-coordinate
3. Initialize max_left_value = -infinity
4. Initialize clique_size = 0
5. For each point in sorted order:
   - If (x_j - w_j) >= max_left_value:
     - Add this point to clique
     - Update max_left_value = max(max_left_value, x_j + w_j)
6. Return clique_size

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by x-coordinate
points.sort()

max_left_value = float('-inf')
clique_size = 0

for x, w in points:
    right_val = x - w
    left_val = x + w
    
    if right_val >= max_left_value:
        clique_size += 1
        max_left_value = max(max_left_value, left_val)

print(clique_size)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The clique problem is one of the most well-known NP-complete problems. Under some simplification it can be formulated as follows. Consider an undirected graph G. It is required to find a subset of vertices C of the maximum size such that any two of them are connected by an edge in graph G. Sounds simple, doesn't it? Nobody yet knows an algorithm that finds a solution to this problem in polynomial time of the size of the graph. However, as with many other NP-complete problems, the clique problem is easier if you consider a specific type of a graph.

Consider n distinct points on a line. Let the i-th point have the coordinate x_{i} and weight w_{i}. Let's form graph G, whose vertices are these points and edges connect exactly the pairs of points (i, j), such that the distance between them is not less than the sum of their weights, or more formally: |x_{i} - x_{j}| ≥ w_{i} + w_{j}.

Find the size of the maximum clique in such graph.


-----Input-----

The first line contains the integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 200 000) — the number of points.

Each of the next n lines contains two numbers x_{i}, w_{i} (0 ≤ x_{i} ≤ 10^9, 1 ≤ w_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the coordinate and the weight of a point. All x_{i} are different.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of vertexes in the maximum clique of the given graph.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
2 3
3 1
6 1
0 2

Output
3



-----Note-----

If you happen to know how to solve this problem without using the specific properties of the graph formulated in the problem statement, then you are able to get a prize of one million dollars!

Actually, standard longest non-decreasing subsequence can be found in O(n log n) using binary search.

The algorithm:
- Process points in order
- Maintain an array "tails" where tails[i] is the smallest ending value of a non-decreasing subsequence of length i+1
- For each new value v, find the leftmost position where v >= tails[i], and update it

But I need the longest non-decreasing subsequence.

Actually, I can use the following approach:
- Sort points by x
- For each point, compute t = x + w
- Find longest non-decreasing subsequence of t values

```python
import bisect

n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by x-coordinate
points.sort()

# Extract t values: t_i = x_i + w_i
t_values = [x + w for x, w in points]

# Find longest non-decreasing subsequence
# Use patience sorting approach
tails = []  # tails[i] = smallest ending value of non-decreasing subsequence of length i+1

for t in t_values:
    # Find the leftmost position where t >= tails[i]
    pos = bisect.bisect_left(tails, t)
    if pos == len(tails):
        tails.append(t)
    else:
        tails[pos] = t

print(len(tails))
```

Wait, is this working? Let me trace through the example:

(2,3), (3,1), (6,1), (0,2)

t_values: 
- (0,2): 0+2 = 2
- (2,3): 2+3 = 5  
- (3,1): 3+1 = 4
- (6,1): 6+1 = 7

t_values = [2, 5, 4, 7]

Process:
- t=2: tails = [2]
- t=5: 5 >= 2, so pos = 1, tails = [2, 5]
- t=4: 4 < 5, pos = bisect_left([2,5], 4) = 1, so tails[1] = 4 → tails = [2, 4]
- t=7: 7 >= 4, pos = 2, tails = [2, 4, 7]

Length = 3.

Output = 3 ✓

This matches the expected answer.

The algorithm works.

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by coordinate
points.sort()

# Create array of s_i = x_i + w_i
s_values = [x + w for x, w in points]

# Find length of longest non-decreasing subsequence
# We can do this with a simpler approach: iterate and maintain the maximum length
# Actually, we can use the fact that we can greedily extend

# Simple O(n^2) won't work for 200,000
# Use O(n log n) algorithm with coordinate compression

# Alternative: since non-decreasing, we can use a greedy method that's essentially
# the longest increasing subsequence but allowing equal values

# Standard approach: use a list to track the end of each subsequence
# But we can optimize

# Actually, for longest non-decreasing subsequence, we can do:
# Use a list to store the last element of each possible length
# For each element, find the first element in the list that is >= current value
# If found, replace it; otherwise append

# But since we want non-decreasing, we can use:

from bisect import bisect_right

# We'll store the last element of the longest non-decreasing subsequence ending at each position
# Actually, we want to maintain a list where each element is the end of a non-decreasing subsequence
# and the list is non-decreasing

# Standard algorithm for longest non-decreasing subsequence:
# We maintain an array `tails` where tails[i] is the smallest ending element of a non-decreasing subsequence of length i+1
# For each element, we find the leftmost position where it can extend or replace

tails = []
for val in s_values:
    # Find the leftmost position where tails[pos] >= val
    # Since tails is non-decreasing, we want the first position >= val
    pos = bisect_right(tails, val)  # This gives first position > val, but we want >=
    # Actually, bisect_right returns the insertion point after the last val
    # We want the first position where tails[pos] >= val
    # bisect_left(tails, val) gives first position >= val
    
    pos = bisect_left(tails, val)
    if pos == len(tails):
        tails.append(val)
    else:
        tails[pos] = val

print(len(tails))
```

Wait, actually I think I have a confusion. Let me double-check the standard algorithm.

For longest increasing subsequence (LIS), we use:
- tails[i] = the smallest tail of an increasing subsequence of length i+1
- For each element, we find the leftmost position where tails[pos] >= current value, and replace it.

But for non-decreasing (allowing equal values), we can use the same algorithm since equal values are allowed.

The algorithm above is correct for non-decreasing subsequence.

Let me verify with the example:
- s_values = [2,5,4,7]
- Process 2: tails = [2]
- Process 5: bisect_left(tails,5) = 1, len(tails)=1, so pos=1==len(tails), append → tails=[2,5]
- Process 4: bisect_left(tails,4) = 1 (since 4 >= 2 but 4 < 5), so pos=1, replace tails[1]=4 → tails=[2,4]
- Process 7: bisect_left(tails,7)=2, len(tails)=2, append → tails=[2,4,7]

Length = 3 ✓

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

The palindrome is a string that can be read the same way from left to right and from right to left. For example, strings "aaaaa", "1221", "bbaabb" are palindromes, however the string "chef" is not a palindrome because if we read it from right to left, we will obtain "fehc" that is not the same as "chef".

We call a string a "double string" if it has an even length and the first half of this string is equal to the second half of this string, for example "abab" is a double string because the first half "ab" is equal to the second half "ab", however the string "abba" is not a double string because the first half "ab" is not equal to the second half "ba". The empty string "" is a double string, and its length is 0.

Chef doesn't like palindromes, however he likes "double strings". He often likes to change the order of letters in some palindrome and sometimes to remove some symbols from it. Now he wonders: if a palindrome of length N is given, what is the maximal possible number of characters in a "double string" 
that can be obtained by removing and changing the order of symbols in it?

------ Input ------ 

Several test cases are given.

The first line of the sample input contains an integer T - the number of test cases.

Then, T lines follow.

Each line consists of a single integer N - the length of a palindrome.

------ Output ------ 

For each test case output a single integer - answer to the problem.

------ Constraints ------ 

$1≤T≤10000$
$1≤N≤1000000000$

----- Sample Input 1 ------ 
2
2
4
----- Sample Output 1 ------ 
2

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a palindrome of length N
2. We want to create the longest possible "double string" by removing and rearranging characters
3. A double string has even length and both halves are identical
4. We need to find the maximum possible length of such a double string

## Key Insights

1. A palindrome has symmetric properties - characters at position i from the start equal characters at position i from the end.

2. For a double string, we need two identical halves. If we have a double string of length 2k, we need k characters for the first half and k characters for the second half.

3. Since we can remove and rearrange characters freely, we need to figure out how many characters we can use from the palindrome to form a double string.

4. In a palindrome of length N, we have pairs of identical characters at symmetric positions. For example, in "abcba" of length 5, we have pairs (a,a), (b,b), and one middle character c.

5. Each pair of identical characters (like two 'a's) can be used to form one character in both halves of a double string.

## Analysis

Let's think about what characters we can use:
- In any palindrome, characters that appear at symmetric positions are equal
- So if we count the frequency of each character, we can form pairs
- For each pair of identical characters, we can use them to form one character in each half of our double string

For a palindrome of length N:
- The total number of character pairs (where each pair consists of two identical characters) depends on the frequencies
- However, in a palindrome, characters at symmetric positions are equal, so we can count how many "pairs" we can form

Wait, let me reconsider more carefully:

In a palindrome, we have character frequencies such that:
- If N is even: all characters appear in symmetric pairs
- If N is odd: one character appears once (in the middle), others appear in pairs

But for forming a double string, we only care about creating identical halves.

The key insight: since we can rearrange freely, we want to maximize the number of characters we can use to form two identical halves.

Each character in a half contributes to both halves, so we need to have characters that can be duplicated.

In a palindrome of length N, the maximum number of characters we can use in pairs is N, but we need them to form equal halves.

Actually, let's think differently:

To form a double string of length 2k, we need k identical characters in each half. So we need 2k characters total.

```python
T = int(input())
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a palindrome of length N
2. We want to create the longest "double string" possible by removing and rearranging characters
3. A double string has even length and its first half equals its second half

## Key Insights

1. Since we start with a palindrome, we know that characters appear in symmetric positions
2. In any palindrome, if character c appears at position i, it also appears at position (N-1-i)
3. This means each character appears in pairs (except for the middle if N is odd)

## Analysis

For a palindrome of length N:
- If N is even: we have N/2 pairs of identical characters (each pair at symmetric positions)
- If N is odd: we have (N-1)/2 pairs + 1 middle character

For a double string:
- We need to form pairs of characters such that the first half equals the second half
- For a double string of length 2k, we need k pairs of identical characters

Since we can rearrange characters arbitrarily, we want to maximize the number of characters in our double string.

The key insight: in a palindrome, each character appears in symmetric pairs. So we can use each pair of identical characters to form a pair in our double string.

For each character, if it appears in pairs (which it does in the palindrome), we can use those pairs to build our double string.

## Case Analysis

**Case 1: N is even**
- N = 2k
- Number of character pairs = k
- We can form a double string of length 2k (using all pairs)
- Answer = N

**Case 2: N is odd**
- N = 2k + 1
- Number of character pairs = k
- We can form a double string of length 2k (using k pairs)
- Answer = 2k = N - 1

Wait, let me reconsider. Since we're forming a double string where the first half equals the second half, we need to form pairs that can be duplicated.

In a palindrome, each character appears in symmetric positions. So every character appears in at least one pair. The total number of "character pairs" available is floor(N/2).

For a double string of length 2k, we need k pairs of identical characters. Since we can rearrange freely, we can use any of the available character pairs.

The maximum double string length is 2 × (number of character pairs available) = 2 × floor(N/2).

So:
- If N is even: answer = 2 × (N/2) = N
- If N is odd: answer = 2 × ((N-1)/2) = N - 1

## Verification with examples

N = 2 (even): answer = 2 ✓
N = 4 (even): answer = 4 ✓

Let me check with N = 3 (odd):
- Palindrome of length 3: e.g., "aba"
- Character pairs: 'a' appears in 2 positions (symmetric), 'b' appears once
- Available pairs: 1 (from 'a')
- Can form double string of length 2 (using 'a' twice)
- Answer should be 2 = 3 - 1 ✓

## Final Answer

The maximum length of double string = 2 × floor(N/2) = N if N is even, N-1 if N is odd.

This simplifies to: N if N even, N-1 if N odd.

In code: 2 * (N // 2)

```python
T = int(input())
for _ in range(T):
    N = int(input())
    print(2 * (N // 2))
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a palindrome of length N
2. We want to create the longest possible "double string" by removing and reordering characters
3. A "double string" has even length and its first half equals its second half

## Key Insights

1. **Palindromes have symmetric properties**: In a palindrome, characters appear in a symmetric pattern. If we have a palindrome of length N, the character at position i from the start equals the character at position i from the end.

2. **What characters can we use?**: Since we can reorder freely, we can use any character that appears in the palindrome.

3. **How many characters can form a double string?**: For a double string of length 2k, we need k characters in the first half and k identical characters in the second half. So we need at least 2k instances of the same character (k in first half, k in second half).

4. **What's the maximum possible double string?**: Since we start with a palindrome, we need to figure out how many character pairs we can form.

## Analysis of Palindromes

For a palindrome of length N:
- If N is odd: there's one middle character that appears once (or with frequency 1), and the rest form symmetric pairs.
- If N is even: all characters form symmetric pairs.

The key insight is that in any palindrome, the frequency of each character has a specific pattern:
- Characters that are at symmetric positions appear in pairs
- The middle character (if N is odd) appears once

Since we can reorder freely and we want to maximize the double string, we need to find how many pairs of identical characters we can form.

In a palindrome, the number of character pairs is limited by the total number of characters that can form symmetric pairs.

## Key Realization

For any palindrome of length N, we can always form a double string of length N (the entire palindrome) by rearranging it optimally?

Wait, let me reconsider. A double string requires that the first half equals the second half. So we need to form pairs of identical characters.

Since we start with a palindrome, the maximum number of identical character pairs we can form is limited by the total count of each character.

But here's the crucial point: in a palindrome, characters come in pairs (except possibly one middle character). So we can use all the characters to form a double string, but we need to find the maximum even length.

Actually, let me think differently:

Since we can reorder freely, and we have a palindrome, we can sort all characters and form the maximum double string.

The maximum double string length will be determined by how many identical pairs we can form.

## Analysis of Palindromes

A palindrome has symmetric properties:
- If N is odd, there's one character in the middle (unchanged)
- If N is even, all characters are symmetric

Since we can rearrange freely, we don't care about the original palindrome structure - we only care about the total counts of characters.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

We have an undirected graph with (N_1+N_2) vertices and M edges.  For i=1,2,\ldots,M, the i-th edge connects vertex a_i and vertex b_i.

The following properties are guaranteed:

- Vertex u and vertex v are connected, for all integers u and v with 1 \leq u,v \leq N_1.
- Vertex u and vertex v are connected, for all integers u and v with N_1+1 \leq u,v \leq N_1+N_2.
- Vertex 1 and vertex (N_1+N_2) are disconnected.

Consider performing the following operation exactly once:

- choose an integer u with 1 \leq u \leq N_1 and an integer v with N_1+1 \leq v \leq N_1+N_2, and add an edge connecting vertex u and vertex v.

We can show that vertex 1 and vertex (N_1+N_2) are always connected in the resulting graph; so let d be the minimum length (number of edges) of a path between vertex 1 and vertex (N_1+N_2).  
Find the maximum possible d resulting from adding an appropriate edge to add.

Definition of "connected"

Two vertices u and v of an undirected graph are said to be connected if and only if there is a path between vertex u and vertex v.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N_1 N_2 M

a_1 b_1

\vdots

a_M b_M

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N_1,N_2 \leq 1.5 \times 10^5
- 0 \leq M \leq 3 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq a_i \leq b_i \leq N_1+N_2
- (a_i,b_i) \neq (a_j,b_j) if i \neq j.
- Vertex u and vertex v are connected for all integers u and v such that 1 \leq u,v \leq N_1.
- Vertex u and vertex v are connected for all integers u and v such that N_1+1 \leq u,v \leq N_1+N_2.
- Vertex 1 and vertex (N_1+N_2) are disconnected.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3 4 6

1 2

2 3

4 5

4 6

1 3

6 7

Sample Output 1

5


If we set u=2 and v=5, the operation yields d=5, which is the maximum possible.

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an undirected graph with (N₁ + N₂) vertices and M edges
2. The first N₁ vertices (1 to N₁) form a connected component
3. The last N₂ vertices (N₁+1 to N₁+N₂) form a connected component  
4. Vertex 1 and vertex (N₁+N₂) are initially disconnected
5. We add exactly one edge between a vertex u (1 ≤ u ≤ N₁) and vertex v (N₁+1 ≤ v ≤ N₁+N₂)
6. After adding this edge, we want to maximize the shortest path distance between vertex 1 and vertex (N₁+N₂)

## Key Insights

1. After adding an edge between u and v, the shortest path from 1 to (N₁+N₂) will be:
   - Distance from 1 to u + 1 (for the new edge) + distance from v to (N₁+N₂)

2. Since the first N₁ vertices are connected, we can reach any vertex in the first component from vertex 1 with some distance.
3. Similarly, we can reach any vertex in the second component from vertex (N₁+N₂) with some distance.
4. To maximize the shortest path, we need to maximize: `dist(1, u) + 1 + dist(v, N₁+N₂)`

5. Since we're adding one edge, we need to find the optimal pair (u,v) that maximizes this sum.

6. We need to precompute:
   - The shortest distance from vertex 1 to all vertices in the first component (1 to N₁)
   - The shortest distance from vertex (N₁+N₂) to all vertices in the second component (N₁+1 to N₁+N₂)

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build the graph from input
2. Use BFS from vertex 1 to compute distances to all vertices in first component (1 to N₁)
3. Use BFS from vertex (N₁+N₂) to compute distances to all vertices in second component (N₁+1 to N₁+N₂)
4. For each possible edge (u,v) where u ∈ [1,N₁] and v ∈ [N₁+1, N₁+N₂]:
   - Calculate distance = dist1[u] + 1 + dist2[v]
5. Return the maximum such distance

## Implementation

```python
from collections import deque, defaultdict

def solve():
    N1, N2, M = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    edges = []
    
    for _ in range(M):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        graph[a].append(b)
        graph[b].append(a)
        edges.append((a, b))
    
    # BFS from vertex 1 to compute distances in first component (1 to N1)
    dist1 = [-1] * (N1 + N2 + 1)
    queue = deque([1])
    dist1[1] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if dist1[v] == -1:
                dist1[v] = dist1[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # BFS from vertex (N1+N2) to compute distances in second component (N1+1 to N1+N2)
    dist2 = [-1] * (N1 + N2 + 1)
    queue = deque([N1 + N2])
    dist2[N1 + N2] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if dist2[v] == -1:
                dist2[v] = dist2[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Find the maximum possible distance
    max_dist = 0
    
    # Check all possible edges between first and second components
    for u in range(1, N1 + 1):
        if dist1[u] != -1:  # u is in first component (should be reachable from 1)
            for v in range(N1 + 1, N1 + N2 + 1):
                if dist2[v] != -1:  # v is in second component (should be reachable from N1+N2)
                    distance = dist1[u] + 1 + dist2[v]
                    max_dist = max(max_dist, distance)
    
    print(max_dist)

solve()
```

Wait, I need to be more careful with the edge selection. Since the graph is large (up to 1.5e5 vertices), checking all pairs (N1 × N2) would be too slow in the worst case (up to 2.25e10 pairs).

Let me optimize: we need to find the maximum value of `dist1[u] + dist2[v] + 1` over all possible pairs (u,v).

This is equivalent to finding: `max(dist1[u]) + max(dist2[v]) + 1`

So the maximum distance is simply the maximum distance from 1 to any vertex in first component plus the maximum distance from (N₁+N₂) to any vertex in second component, plus 1.

```python
from collections import deque, defaultdict

def solve():
    N1, N2, M = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    
    for _ in range(M):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        graph[a].append(b)
        graph[b].append(a)
    
    # BFS from vertex 1 to compute distances in first component (1 to N1)
    dist1 = [-1] * (N1 + N2 + 1)
    queue = deque([1])
    dist1[1] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if dist1[v] == -1:
                dist1[v] = dist1[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # BFS from vertex (N1+N2) to compute distances in second component (N1+1 to N1+N2)
    dist2 = [-1] * (N1 + N2 + 1)
    queue = deque([N1 + N2])
    dist2[N1 + N2] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if dist2[v] == -1:
                dist2[v] = dist2[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Find maximum distances in each component
    max_dist1 = 0
    for i in range(1, N1 + 1):
        max_dist1 = max(max_dist1, dist1[i])
    
    max_dist2 = 0
    for i in range(N1 + 1, N1 + N2 + 1):
        max_dist2 = max(max_dist2, dist2[i])
    
    result = max_dist1 + max_dist2 + 1
    print(result)

solve()
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

We have an undirected graph with (N_1+N_2) vertices and M edges.  For i=1,2,\ldots,M, the i-th edge connects vertex a_i and vertex b_i.

The following properties are guaranteed:

- Vertex u and vertex v are connected, for all integers u and v with 1 \leq u,v \leq N_1.
- Vertex u and vertex v are connected, for all integers u and v with N_1+1 \leq u,v \leq N_1+N_2.
- Vertex 1 and vertex (N_1+N_2) are disconnected.

Consider performing the following operation exactly once:

- choose an integer u with 1 \leq u \leq N_1 and an integer v with N_1+1 \leq v \leq N_1+N_2, and add an edge connecting vertex u and vertex v.

We can show that vertex 1 and vertex (N_1+N_2) are always connected in the resulting graph; so let d be the minimum length (number of edges) of a path between vertex 1 and vertex (N_1+N_2).  
Find the maximum possible d resulting from adding an appropriate edge to add.

Definition of "connected"

Two vertices u and v of an undirected graph are said to be connected if and only if there is a path between vertex u and vertex v.

- Vertex u and vertex v are connected for all integers u and v such that N_1+1 \leq u,v \leq N_1+N_2.
- Vertex 1 and vertex (N_1+N_2) are disconnected.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an undirected graph with (N₁ + N₂) vertices and M edges
2. The first N₁ vertices form a connected component (all vertices 1 to N₁ are connected)
3. The last N₂ vertices form a connected component (all vertices N₁+1 to N₁+N₂ are connected)
4. Vertex 1 and vertex (N₁+N₂) are initially disconnected
5. We must add exactly one edge between a vertex u (1 ≤ u ≤ N₁) and a vertex v (N₁+1 ≤ v ≤ N₁+N₂)
6. After adding this edge, we want to maximize the shortest path distance between vertex 1 and vertex (N₁+N₂)

## Key Insights

1. After adding an edge between u ∈ [1, N₁] and v ∈ [N₁+1, N₁+N₂], the shortest path from 1 to (N₁+N₂) will be:
   - Distance from 1 to u + 1 + distance from v to (N₁+N₂)

2. We need to find the maximum possible value of this expression over all possible choices of u and v.

3. Since we want to maximize the distance, we should:
   - Choose u that maximizes the distance from 1 to u
   - Choose v that maximizes the distance from v to (N₁+N₂)

4. In a connected graph, the maximum distance between two vertices is the diameter of the component.

## Algorithm Steps

1. Find the distance from vertex 1 to all other vertices in the first N₁ components using BFS
2. Find the distance from each vertex in the last N₂ components to the vertex (N₁+N₂) using BFS
3. For each possible edge addition (u,v), calculate the distance as: dist(1,u) + 1 + dist(v, N₁+N₂)
4. Return the maximum of these values

## Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def bfs_distance(graph, start, n):
    """Return distances from start to all vertices in range [1, n]"""
    dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
    queue = deque([start])
    dist[start] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if dist[v] == -1:
                dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    return dist

def solve():
    N1, N2, M = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build the graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    edges = []
    
    for _ in range(M):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        graph[a].append(b)
        graph[b].append(a)
        edges.append((a, b))
    
    # Find distances from vertex 1 to all vertices in first component (1 to N1)
    dist_from_1 = bfs_distance(graph, 1, N1)
    
    # Find distances from each vertex in second component (N1+1 to N1+N2) to vertex (N1+N2)
    dist_to_end = bfs_distance(graph, N1 + N2, N1 + N2)
    
    # For each possible edge (u, v) where u in [1, N1] and v in [N1+1, N1+N2]
    # The distance will be: dist(1,u) + 1 + dist(v, N1+N2)
    max_distance = 0
    
    # We can optimize by finding the maximum possible distances
    # Instead of checking all combinations, we find:
    # - max dist from 1 to any u in [1, N1]
    # - max dist from v to (N1+N2) for any v in [N1+1, N1+N2]
    
    max_dist_to_u = max(dist_from_1[1:N1+1])
    max_dist_to_v = max(dist_to_end[N1+1:N1+N2+1])
    
    # The maximum distance will be the sum of these maximums plus 1
    max_distance = max_dist_to_u + max_dist_to_v + 1
    
    print(max_distance)

solve()
```

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def bfs_distances(graph, start, n):
    """Return distances from start to all vertices from 1 to n"""
    dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
    queue = deque([start])
    dist[start] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if dist[v] == -1:
                dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    return dist

def solve():
    N1, N2, M = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build the graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    
    for _ in range(M):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        graph[a].append(b)
        graph[b].append(a)

    dist_from_1 = bfs_distances(graph, 1, N1 + N2)
    
    # Find distances from vertex (N1+N2) to all vertices in second component (N1+1 to N1+N2)
    dist_to_end = bfs_distances(graph, N1 + N2, N1 + N2)

    # The distance will be: dist(1,u) + 1 + dist(v, N1+N2)

                distance = dist_from_1[u] + 1 + dist_to_end[v]
                max_distance = max(max_distance, distance)
```

```python
def bfs_distances(graph, start, n):
    """Return distances from start to all vertices from 1 to n"""
    dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
```
